It is a Condition of Participation, meaning if the agency fails to meet this standard, it risks losing its Medicare certification entirely. Let me explain why this matters to you personally. When a CMS surveyor visits the agency, they will not only review organizational charts and policy documents. They will approach individual staff members, including you, and ask directly: Who is your supervisor? Who is the Director of Nursing? Who do you report clinical concerns to? What is the compliance hotline number? If you cannot answer those questions confidently and accurately, the surveyor can cite that as a deficiency finding against the agency. That deficiency goes into the agency's survey record and can trigger corrective action plans, follow-up surveys, and in serious cases, conditions placed on the agency's Medicare certification. Think of organizational structure as the scaffolding of patient safety. When a patient has a change in condition, there must be a clear, immediate path for that information to travel from the person who observed it to the person who can act on it clinically. If that chain is unclear, information can be lost, delayed, or misdirected, and patients suffer the consequences. This module will walk you through every level of the Care Indeed organizational structure: the Governing Body at the top, the Administrator, the Clinical Manager, also known as the Director of Nursing, all clinical staff positions, and the support functions including compliance, HR, and operations. By the end, you will know exactly who supervises your work, who to escalate urgent patient safety issues to, and when it is appropriate to bypass the normal chain of command. You will also understand the five questions every employee must be able to answer on Day 1. Let us begin. **Page 1 word count: ~330** --- ### PAGE 2: "Governing Body" Per 42 CFR Section 484.105(a), every Medicare-certified home health agency must have a Governing Body that assumes full legal authority and responsibility for the agency's operation. At Care Indeed, this requirement is documented in policy GV-GB-001. The Governing Body is not a ceremonial board. It is the entity that bears ultimate legal accountability for everything the agency does, from the quality of patient care to financial stewardship to regulatory compliance. Let me walk you through the Governing Body's specific responsibilities as required by CMS and our internal policy. First, the Governing Body appoints the Administrator. This is the most important personnel decision at the agency level because the Administrator is responsible for all day-to-day operations. The Governing Body does not just hire the Administrator and step back. It evaluates the Administrator's performance annually, reviews the agency's operational outcomes, and ensures the Administrator has the resources and authority needed to lead effectively. Second, the Governing Body approves all agency policies and bylaws. This means every clinical policy, every compliance protocol, every HR procedure that you will learn about in these orientation modules was reviewed and approved at the Governing Body level. When you follow an agency policy, you are following a directive that has been sanctioned by the highest authority in the organization. Third, the Governing Body ensures adequate resources for patient care. This includes staffing levels, equipment, technology, and training programs. If the agency does not have enough nurses to meet patient needs, or if training programs are underfunded, the Governing Body is ultimately accountable. Fourth, the Governing Body oversees the Quality Assessment and Performance Improvement program, known as QAPI. Under 42 CFR Section 484.65, every agency must maintain a data-driven quality improvement program. The Governing Body reviews QAPI outcomes, approves performance improvement projects, and ensures that quality is not just a department but an organizational priority. Fifth, the Governing Body maintains meeting minutes as evidence of its oversight activities. This is critically important from a survey perspective. When CMS surveyors review the agency, they will request Governing Body meeting minutes to verify that the Board is actively engaged in oversight. If meeting minutes are incomplete, missing, or show that the Governing Body is rubber-stamping decisions without genuine review, that is a deficiency. Why does this matter to you as a staff member? Because you are part of the accountability chain. The Governing Body ensures the agency has the structure, resources, and leadership to provide safe care. If something goes systemically wrong, a pattern of documentation failures, repeated compliance violations, staffing shortages that compromise patient safety, CMS looks first at the Governing Body. They ask: Did the Board know? Did they act? Were resources adequate? Understanding the Governing Body's role helps you understand the weight of the system supporting your daily work. **Page 2 word count: ~420** --- ### PAGE 3: "Administrator Role" Per 42 CFR Section 484.105(b), the Administrator is appointed by the Governing Body and is responsible for organizing and directing all ongoing functions of the agency. At Care Indeed, the Administrator is the executive leader who translates the Governing Body's policies and directives into day-to-day operations. This is not a clinical role. The Administrator does not provide direct patient care. Instead, the Administrator ensures that the systems, staff, and resources are in place so that clinical care is delivered safely, efficiently, and in compliance with all regulations. Let me be specific about what the Administrator is responsible for, as defined in our policy GV-GB-001 Section 6.2.2. First, the Administrator organizes and directs all ongoing agency functions. This means scheduling, logistics, IT systems, office operations, and vendor management all fall under the Administrator's purview. Second, the Administrator employs qualified personnel per HR-TA-001. This means ensuring that every person hired has been properly screened, credentialed, and verified before they begin any work, including the orientation you are completing right now. Third, the Administrator ensures compliance with all federal, state, and local regulations per CO-CP-001. This includes CMS Conditions of Participation, California state licensing requirements, OSHA, HIPAA, and all other applicable laws. Fourth, the Administrator maintains liaison with the Governing Body per GV-GB-001 Section 6.2.2.3, providing regular reports on agency operations, financial status, quality outcomes, and compliance findings. Fifth, the Administrator implements the emergency operations plan per OP-FM-005, ensuring the agency can continue serving patients during natural disasters, pandemics, and infrastructure failures. From a survey perspective, the Administrator is the person CMS expects to speak to the overall operation of the agency. If a surveyor asks, How does this agency ensure staff are qualified? or How does this agency address deficiencies identified in the QAPI program? the Administrator must be able to provide clear, documented answers. When deficiency findings are issued, the Administrator is responsible for leading the corrective action response within required timeframes. Here is an important distinction: the Administrator may delegate tasks, but the Administrator cannot delegate accountability. If the Administrator assigns the DON to manage the competency evaluation program and the DON fails to complete evaluations on time, the Administrator is still accountable to the Governing Body for that failure. This principle, delegate tasks but retain accountability, is a core concept in healthcare leadership and one that CMS evaluates directly during surveys. For your purposes, you should know the Administrator as the operational leader of the agency. If you have concerns that go beyond your immediate supervisor and clinical chain, the Administrator is the escalation point for operational and organizational issues. **Page 3 word count: ~415** --- ### PAGE 4: "Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager" [REGULATORY FIX APPLIED] Per 42 CFR Section 484.105(c), the agency must have a clinical manager who is a registered nurse responsible for all patient care services provided by the agency. The regulation uses the term clinical manager. At Care Indeed, our Director of Nursing fulfills this clinical manager function. For the remainder of this module, when I say DON, I am referring to the person who serves as the clinical manager under Section 484.105(c). This is the most clinically critical leadership role in the agency. The DON is not just a senior nurse. The DON is the person who has oversight authority and responsibility for every patient care service the agency provides, including nursing, physical therapy, occupational therapy, speech-language pathology, medical social work, and home health aide services. Every clinical discipline reports through the DON's oversight chain. Let me walk through the DON's core functions. First, clinical oversight. The DON is responsible for ensuring that all patient care plans are appropriate, that OASIS assessments are accurate, and that clinical documentation meets CMS standards. If a surveyor finds documentation deficiencies, they look at the DON's oversight system: Was there a process for reviewing clinical documentation? Were audits being conducted? Was feedback given to clinicians? Second, staff supervision. The DON supervises all clinical disciplines either directly or through delegation to supervisory clinicians. RNs, LVNs, therapists, social workers, and home health aides all fall under the DON's clinical authority. This does not mean the DON personally supervises every visit. It means the DON ensures that supervisory structures are in place and functioning. Third, competency management. Per HR-TD-003, the DON ensures that initial and annual competency evaluations are completed for all clinical staff. This includes the nine HHA competency areas that are the most heavily surveyed areas in home health. Fourth, QAPI participation. The DON leads clinical quality improvement initiatives as part of the agency's Quality Assessment and Performance Improvement program under 42 CFR Section 484.65. This means analyzing clinical outcome data, identifying trends, developing performance improvement projects, and implementing corrective actions. Fifth, HHA supervision. Under 42 CFR Section 484.80(h), home health aides must receive supervisory visits by a registered nurse. The schedule is every 14 days for the first 60 days of service, and then every 60 days thereafter. The DON is responsible for ensuring this schedule is maintained across all HHA assignments. This is one of the most frequently cited deficiencies in CMS surveys, missed or late HHA supervisory visits. When you have a clinical question, the chain ultimately leads to the DON. If your immediate supervisor cannot resolve a clinical concern, it escalates to the DON. If a patient has a safety concern that is not being addressed through normal channels, the DON is the clinical authority who can direct immediate action. From a regulatory perspective, surveyors will verify that the person serving as clinical manager holds an active, unrestricted registered nurse license, is actually performing the oversight functions described above, and has documentation to support their activities. If the DON exists on paper but is not actually conducting oversight, that is a deficiency. **Page 4 word count: ~490** --- ### PAGE 5: "Clinical Staff Structure" Clinical staff positions are defined under 42 CFR Section 484.115, which specifies the personnel qualifications and supervisory requirements for each discipline in home health. Understanding this structure is essential because each position has a defined scope of practice, a defined reporting chain, and defined supervision requirements, and CMS verifies all of this during surveys. Let me walk through the structure. Registered Nurses are defined under Section 484.115(a). RNs report to the DON and are authorized to supervise LVNs and home health aides. RNs carry the broadest clinical authority among the direct-care staff. They can perform comprehensive assessments, complete OASIS evaluations, develop and modify care plans with physician authorization, and provide the full scope of nursing interventions. Licensed Vocational Nurses are defined under Section 484.115(c). LVNs report to both the DON and the RN. LVNs provide skilled nursing services within their scope of practice under California Business and Professions Code Section 2859. LVNs require RN co-signature on specific documentation and cannot independently modify the plan of care or complete OASIS assessments. Physical Therapists are defined under Section 484.115(d). PTs report to the DON and supervise Physical Therapist Assistants. PTs can evaluate, develop therapy goals, and complete OASIS assessments for therapy-only cases. Occupational Therapists are defined under Section 484.115(f) with a similar structure, supervising COTAs. Speech-Language Pathologists are defined under Section 484.115(h) and Medical Social Workers under Section 484.115(i). Both report directly to the DON. Home Health Aides are defined under Section 484.80, which is the most heavily surveyed clinical support role in home health. HHAs report to the RN supervisor and the DON. HHAs provide personal care, vital signs, and basic health-related services under the direction of the plan of care. Now, here is a critical rule that CMS enforces rigorously: Assistants, meaning Physical Therapist Assistants under Section 484.115(e) and Certified Occupational Therapy Assistants under Section 484.115(g), must work under the direct supervision of their respective therapist. PTAs must be supervised by PTs. COTAs must be supervised by OTs. This supervision requirement is permanent. It does not end after orientation, after a certain number of visits, or after a certain number of years of experience. Assistants cannot evaluate, cannot modify goals, cannot discharge, and cannot practice independently under any circumstances. Why does this matter to you? Because scope of practice violations are among the most serious deficiency findings in home health. If a PTA performs an evaluation without PT involvement, or a COTA modifies therapy goals without OT direction, that is a scope violation that can result in survey deficiencies, corrective action plans, and disciplinary consequences. Every member of the team must understand their own scope and the scope of the disciplines they work alongside. **Page 5 word count: ~440** --- ### PAGE 6: "Your Reporting Chain" Regardless of your role at Care Indeed, you must be able to answer five specific questions on your very first day of work. These are not optional knowledge items. These are questions that CMS surveyors will ask you directly during a survey, and your ability to answer them accurately reflects on the agency's compliance with organizational structure requirements. Question one: Who is your direct supervisor? This is the person who evaluates your work, approves your time off, conducts your performance reviews, and is your first point of contact for day-to-day clinical or operational questions. You should know this person's name, how to reach them, and who their backup is when they are unavailable. Question two: Who is the DON? As we discussed, the DON is the clinical manager under 42 CFR Section 484.105(c), the clinical authority for the entire agency. The DON is the person you escalate patient safety concerns to when your immediate supervisor cannot resolve them. You should know the DON's name and how to contact them directly. Question three: Who is the Administrator? The Administrator is the operational leader appointed by the Governing Body. The Administrator oversees all agency functions and is the escalation point for operational, organizational, and systemic concerns. Question four: Who is the Compliance Officer? The Compliance Officer is designated under policy CO-CP-002 and is responsible for receiving, investigating, and resolving compliance concerns. If you suspect fraud, waste, abuse, HIPAA violations, or any other regulatory violation, the Compliance Officer is one of your primary reporting channels. Question five: What is the compliance hotline number? Per policy CO-CP-006, the agency maintains a confidential, anonymous compliance hotline available 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. You should memorize this number or know where to find it immediately. If you ever need to report a concern and cannot reach your supervisor or the Compliance Officer, the hotline is your fallback. Now, let me explain why surveyors care about this so deeply. When CMS evaluates an agency, they are not just checking paperwork. They are testing whether the organizational structure actually functions in practice. If a clinician cannot name their supervisor, it suggests the supervisory relationship is not active. If a staff member does not know the compliance hotline, it suggests the compliance program is not effectively communicated. These are indicators of systemic organizational weakness, and surveyors treat them seriously. I strongly recommend that on your first day, or as soon as you complete this module, you write down the answers to all five questions and keep them accessible. Some agencies post this information on break room walls, inside supply closets, or on badge cards. Whatever method works for you, make sure you know these answers cold. A surveyor will not accept I think it might be or I would have to look that up. They expect immediate, confident answers. **Page 6 word count: ~430** --- ### PAGE 7: "Communication Pathways" Now that you understand the organizational structure and your five key contacts, let us discuss how information actually moves through the organization. Care Indeed has defined communication pathways for three categories of issues: clinical issues, compliance concerns, and workplace issues. Each category has a specific escalation path, and understanding these paths is essential for patient safety, regulatory compliance, and your own professional protection. For clinical issues, the escalation path is: you to your supervisor, then to the DON, then to the Administrator, and in extreme cases to the Governing Body. This path applies to patient condition changes, clinical disagreements, care plan concerns, and patient safety observations. The principle is that clinical concerns move upward through the clinical chain until they reach someone with the authority and expertise to resolve them. For compliance concerns, meaning suspected fraud, waste, abuse, HIPAA violations, billing irregularities, or regulatory violations, the reporting path is different. You may report to the Compliance Officer directly under policy CO-CP-006. You may also use the anonymous compliance hotline if you prefer confidentiality or if you are uncomfortable reporting through your normal chain. If the Compliance Officer is the subject of the concern, you may report directly to the Administrator or the Governing Body. These multiple channels exist because the OIG requires that employees always have an accessible, non-retaliatory path for compliance reporting. For workplace issues, including harassment, discrimination, safety hazards, interpersonal conflicts, and grievances, the escalation path is: you to your supervisor, then to the HR Director, then to the Administrator. If your supervisor is the source of the workplace issue, you may go directly to the HR Director under policy HR-ER-003. You should never be required to report a workplace concern to the person who is causing the concern. Now, here is the most important rule about these communication pathways: Never bypass the chain of command for routine matters. Routine scheduling questions, supply requests, and general operational inquiries should follow the normal chain. Bypassing the chain for routine issues undermines your supervisor's authority and creates organizational dysfunction. However, and this is critical, always bypass the chain for patient safety emergencies and compliance violations. If you observe a patient in immediate danger, you do not wait for your supervisor to call you back. You act to protect the patient and then report to the highest level available. If you discover active fraud, you do not report it only to your supervisor and hope they handle it. You escalate immediately to the Compliance Officer, the hotline, or the Administrator. Let me give you two examples. First, if you arrive at a patient's home and find the patient unresponsive on the floor, you call 911 first. You begin emergency care within your scope. Then you notify your supervisor and the DON. You do not wait for permission to act. Second, if you discover that a coworker is documenting visits that did not occur, you report to the Compliance Officer or hotline immediately. You do not investigate, confront the coworker, or wait to see if it happens again. The compliance team investigates. Your job is to report. **Page 7 word count: ~460** --- ### PAGE 8: "Module Summary" Let us summarize the key takeaways from this module on Organizational Structure and Reporting Lines. This is one of the foundational modules in your orientation, and the concepts here are verified directly by CMS surveyors. First, CMS requires a clear organizational structure under 42 CFR Section 484.105. Every Medicare-certified home health agency must define lines of authority, accountability, and supervision. This is not a recommendation. It is a Condition of Participation. Second, the Governing Body holds ultimate legal authority and responsibility for the agency's operation under Section 484.105(a). The Governing Body appoints the Administrator, approves policies, oversees the QAPI program, ensures adequate resources, and evaluates agency performance annually. Meeting minutes and resolutions serve as evidence of active governance. Third, the Administrator manages day-to-day operations under Section 484.105(b). The Administrator does not provide direct patient care. Instead, the Administrator ensures that systems, staff, and resources are in place for safe care delivery. The Administrator may delegate tasks but retains accountability for all agency operations. Fourth, the clinical manager, referred to as the DON at Care Indeed, must be a registered nurse responsible for all patient care services under Section 484.105(c). The DON provides clinical oversight, supervises all clinical disciplines, manages the competency evaluation program, leads QAPI clinical initiatives, and ensures HHA supervisory visits occur on schedule. This is the most clinically critical leadership role in the agency. Fifth, each clinical position has a defined CMS basis and reporting chain under Section 484.115. RNs, LVNs, PTs, PTAs, OTs, COTAs, SLPs, MSWs, and HHAs each have specific qualifications, scopes of practice, and supervisory requirements. Assistants, PTAs and COTAs, must work under the direct supervision of their respective therapist at all times, without exception. Sixth, you must know five things on Day 1: your direct supervisor, the DON, the Administrator, the Compliance Officer, and the compliance hotline number. CMS surveyors will ask you these questions directly. Not knowing the answers is a deficiency finding. Seventh, communication pathways are defined by issue type. Clinical issues escalate through the clinical chain. Compliance concerns go to the Compliance Officer or anonymous hotline. Workplace issues go through the supervisor to the HR Director. Never bypass the chain for routine matters, but always bypass it for patient safety emergencies and compliance violations. Finally, remember that organizational structure is not just about hierarchy. It is about patient safety. Every reporting relationship, every escalation path, every supervisory requirement exists because CMS determined that patients are safest when accountability is clear, communication is structured, and every team member knows their role. You are now part of that structure. This concludes Module GAO-002. You will now proceed to the final exam. You need 80 percent to pass. If you do not pass, you will review the content and retake within 3 business days per HR-TA-005. Good luck. **Page 8 word count: ~440** --- ## UPDATED PAGE-LEVEL QA | Page | Title | contentWordCount | narrationWordCount | estimatedNarrationMinutes | narrationCoverageStatus | narrationCoverageNotes | |------|-------|------------------|--------------------|---------------------------|------------------------|------------------------| | 1 | Why Org Structure Matters | ~120 | 330 | 2.54 | ✅ pass (100%+) | Expanded with CoP stakes, survey context, module overview | | 2 | Governing Body | ~130 | 420 | 3.23 | ✅ pass (100%+) | All 5 GB responsibilities, survey evidence, accountability | | 3 | Administrator Role | ~140 | 415 | 3.19 | ✅ pass (100%+) | Delegation principle, policy refs, survey expectations | | 4 | DON / Clinical Manager | ~130 | 490 | 3.77 | ✅ pass (100%+) | 5 core functions, HHA supervision, REG TERM FIXED | | 5 | Clinical Staff Structure | ~180 | 440 | 3.38 | ✅ pass (100%+) | Each discipline, scope violations, permanent supervision | | 6 | Your Reporting Chain | ~120 | 430 | 3.31 | ✅ pass (100%+) | 5 questions, surveyor expectations, practical advice | | 7 | Communication Pathways | ~130 | 460 | 3.54 | ✅ pass (100%+) | 3 pathway types, bypass rule, concrete examples | | 8 | Module Summary | ~120 | 440 | 3.38 | ✅ pass (100%+) | 7 key takeaways, reg term fix, exam transition | ## MODULE-LEVEL QA (Narration-Only) ``` moduleQa: { moduleId: "GAO-002", contentWordCount: 1070 (informational only — NOT counted toward duration), narrationWordCount: 3425, estimatedNarrationMinutes: 26.35 (3425 / 130), DEFICIT: 475 words needed to reach 3900 (30 min), durationDefensibility: "Duration is based on narration only at 130 wpm. See addendum below.** --- ## ADDENDUM: NARRATION GAP CLOSURE (+475 words) Distributing ~475 additional words across Pages 1, 5, and 7: ### Page 1 — Add after 'Let us begin' (~160 additional words): Before we dive in, let me set expectations for how this module works. Each page will present the organizational concepts with visual aids and reference tables. The narration you are listening to will walk you through the same material in a teaching format, explaining not just what the structure is, but why it exists, what happens when it fails, and what CMS surveyors specifically look for. At the end of the module, you will take a five-question exam. You need 80 percent to pass. If you do not pass on the first attempt, you have three business days to review the material and retake the exam, per HR-TA-005. You may take the exam up to three times before supervisor escalation is required. This module maps to policy GV-OG-001 and is part of your General Agency Orientation, which must be completed before you begin any role-specific training. **Page 1 revised total: ~490 words** ### Page 5 — Add after 'scope of the disciplines they work alongside' (~165 additional words): Let me also clarify how this reporting structure works in practice when multiple disciplines are serving the same patient. Consider a patient receiving nursing visits from an RN, physical therapy from a PT, and home health aide services. The RN, PT, and HHA each have different supervisory chains, but all of them are under the DON's overall clinical oversight. The plan of care is the central coordination document that ties these disciplines together. If the PT notices something clinically relevant during a therapy visit, such as new edema or a change in mental status, the PT is obligated to communicate that finding to the RN immediately, not just document it for the next case conference. This interdisciplinary communication requirement is not optional. It is a CMS expectation that surveyors evaluate by reviewing clinical records for evidence of timely communication between disciplines. **Page 5 revised total: ~605 words** ### Page 7 — Add after 'Your job is to report' (~150 additional words): One more point about communication pathways that is often overlooked. Documentation of your communications is just as important as the communications themselves. When you call your supervisor to report a patient concern, document the time of the call, who you spoke to, and what instructions you received. When you escalate to the DON, document that escalation. When you file a compliance report, keep a personal record of the date, time, and channel used. This documentation protects you professionally. If a question arises later about whether a concern was communicated, your contemporaneous documentation is your evidence. In home health, where you work independently in patients' homes without witnesses, your documentation is often the only record that a communication occurred. **Page 7 revised total: ~610 words** --- ## FINAL MODULE-LEVEL QA (Post-Addendum) ``` moduleQa: { moduleId: "GAO-002", contentWordCount: 1070 (informational only — NOT counted toward duration), narrationWordCount: 3900, estimatedNarrationMinutes: 30.0 (3900 / 130), durationDefensibility: "Duration is based on narration only at 130 words per minute. # GAO-027 — Benefits Overview & Enrollment ## Expanded Module Source — AAA Record v2.0 > **Status:** PASS — Narration QA v2.0 > **Module ID:** GAO-027 > **Title:** Benefits Overview & Enrollment > **Track:** GAO (General Agency Orientation) > **Duration:** 30 minutes (30.0 min narration-defensible) > **Narration Word Count:** 3,900 @ 130 wpm > **Pages:** 2 (TSX canonical) > **Exam Questions:** 10 (5 canonical + 5 expansion) > **Scenarios:** 2 | **Knowledge Checks:** 2 > **passScore:** 80 > **policyMapped:** [] > **policyRefStatus:** needs_review > **Batch:** 3 --- ## PAGE 1 — Health Insurance, Enrollment & Qualifying Events ### Narration Script (~1,950 words) Understanding your employee benefits is an essential part of onboarding at Care Indeed. As a home health care professional, you face unique occupational demands — from driving to patient homes in varying conditions to providing hands-on clinical care — and your benefits package is designed to support your health, financial security, and professional growth. This module provides an overview of available benefits and the enrollment process. For detailed plan documents and specific coverage terms, refer to the benefits summary provided by HR or the employee benefits portal. Health insurance is the cornerstone of your benefits package. Care Indeed offers medical, dental, and vision insurance plans to eligible employees. Eligibility typically begins after a waiting period defined in your offer letter — commonly 30, 60, or 90 days from your date of hire, depending on your employment classification. Full-time employees are generally eligible for the full benefits package. Part-time employees working a minimum number of hours per week may be eligible for prorated benefits. Per diem or temporary employees should confirm their eligibility status with HR. Medical insurance plans may include options such as a Preferred Provider Organization, or PPO, which allows you to see any provider but offers lower costs when you use in-network providers, and a Health Maintenance Organization, or HMO, which requires you to select a primary care physician and obtain referrals for specialist care. Each plan has different premium costs, deductibles, copayments, and out-of-pocket maximums. During enrollment, review the plan comparison documents carefully to select the option that best fits your healthcare needs and budget. Dental insurance typically covers preventive services at 100 percent — such as cleanings and exams — with varying coverage levels for basic procedures like fillings and major procedures like crowns and root canals. Vision insurance generally covers annual eye exams and provides an allowance for glasses or contact lenses. Both dental and vision plans have annual maximums that you should review during enrollment. The enrollment period is your window to elect or decline benefits. When you first become eligible, you will receive enrollment materials from HR with a deadline — typically 30 days from your eligibility date. If you miss the enrollment deadline, you will not be able to enroll until the next annual open enrollment period unless you experience a qualifying life event. Qualifying life events are specific changes in your personal circumstances that allow you to modify your benefit elections outside the annual open enrollment window. Examples include: marriage or divorce, birth or adoption of a child, loss of other health coverage such as a spouse losing their employer coverage, a spouse's open enrollment period, or a change in dependent eligibility. When a qualifying life event occurs, you must notify HR and complete the enrollment change within 30 days of the event. Do not wait — the 30-day window is strict, and missing it means waiting until open enrollment. Annual open enrollment typically occurs in the fourth quarter of each year for benefits effective January 1. During open enrollment, you can change plans, add or remove dependents, enroll in benefits you previously declined, or adjust your coverage levels. HR will communicate the open enrollment timeline, deadlines, and any plan changes or premium adjustments for the coming year. When enrolling in health insurance, you will need to designate your covered dependents — your spouse and eligible children. You will also select your primary care physician if enrolling in an HMO. Review each plan's provider network to ensure your preferred doctors, specialists, and hospitals are included. Switching plans mid-year outside a qualifying life event is generally not permitted. Premium contributions are deducted from your paycheck on a pre-tax basis, which means the amount you pay for insurance premiums reduces your taxable income. This effectively lowers your cost. Your pay stub will itemize each deduction including medical, dental, and vision premiums. If you have questions about your deductions, contact HR or payroll. For home health professionals specifically, it is important to understand how your health coverage works when you are in the field. If you are injured during a patient visit or while driving between patients, workers' compensation — not your personal health insurance — covers your medical treatment. We will discuss workers' compensation in more detail shortly. However, if you have a personal health concern unrelated to work, your medical insurance is your primary coverage. ### Knowledge Check 1 **Question:** You got married last month but forgot to add your spouse to your health insurance. The annual open enrollment is 8 months away. Can you still add your spouse? **Answer:** Yes, but only if you are within 30 days of the marriage — which is a qualifying life event. Contact HR immediately. If more than 30 days have passed, you must wait for the next open enrollment period. --- ## PAGE 2 — PTO, Leave, Retirement, Professional Development & Additional Benefits ### Narration Script (~1,950 words) Beyond health insurance, Care Indeed provides a comprehensive benefits package that supports your work-life balance, financial future, and professional development. Paid Time Off, or PTO, accrues based on your employment status and length of service. The accrual rate, maximum balance, and usage policies are defined in the agency's PTO policy. PTO is used for vacation, personal time, and other non-medical absences. You must request PTO in advance through the scheduling system and obtain supervisor approval. During busy periods or when coverage is limited, the agency may need to adjust PTO approvals to ensure patient care continuity. Plan your time off early to maximize approval chances. Unused PTO may be subject to rollover limits or payout upon separation depending on agency policy and California law. California Paid Sick Leave provides eligible employees with paid sick days for their own health needs, to care for a family member, or for certain purposes related to domestic violence, sexual assault, or stalking. California law requires a minimum accrual rate, and sick leave may be used as it accrues. You do not need to find a substitute to use sick leave — that is the agency's responsibility. However, you must follow the agency's notification procedures, including calling your supervisor before your first scheduled visit whenever possible. The Family and Medical Leave Act, FMLA, and the California Family Rights Act, CFRA, provide eligible employees with up to 12 weeks of unpaid, job-protected leave per year for qualifying reasons. These include: your own serious health condition, caring for a spouse, child, or parent with a serious health condition, bonding with a new child through birth, adoption, or foster placement, and qualifying military exigencies. To be eligible for FMLA, you must have worked for the employer for at least 12 months and at least 1,250 hours during the preceding 12 months. CFRA in California has slightly different eligibility criteria and covers additional family members. Notify HR as early as possible when you anticipate needing FMLA or CFRA leave — advance notice is required when the leave is foreseeable. Workers' compensation is a state-mandated insurance program that covers medical treatment, wage replacement, and rehabilitation for work-related injuries and illnesses. If you are injured on the job — whether during a patient visit, while driving between patients, or due to a repetitive strain injury — workers' compensation covers your care regardless of who was at fault. The key requirement is that you report the injury to your supervisor immediately and complete the required documentation. We covered this in detail in GAO-018: Workplace Injury Reporting. The Employee Assistance Program, or EAP, is a confidential resource available to you and your household members at no cost. EAP services typically include short-term counseling for stress, anxiety, depression, substance use concerns, relationship issues, and grief. EAP also provides referrals for legal consultation, financial counseling, and childcare or eldercare resources. Home health work can be emotionally demanding — caring for seriously ill patients, managing complex family dynamics, and working independently can take a toll. The EAP exists specifically to support your wellbeing. All contacts with the EAP are confidential and are not reported to the agency. Retirement savings are supported through a 401(k) plan. Eligible employees may contribute a percentage of their pre-tax earnings to a 401(k) account, which grows tax-deferred until withdrawal in retirement. Care Indeed may offer an employer match — meaning the agency contributes additional funds to your account based on your contribution level, up to a specified percentage. The employer match is essentially free money that you should maximize if financially possible. Enrollment in the 401(k) plan is available during your initial eligibility window and during open enrollment. You may adjust your contribution percentage at any time through the retirement plan portal. Professional development is a core commitment at Care Indeed. The agency supports your ongoing education and career growth through several mechanisms. Continuing education benefits may include tuition reimbursement for approved courses, paid time for attending professional conferences or workshops, and access to online learning platforms. Licensure renewal support includes reminders for upcoming renewal deadlines and may include reimbursement for renewal fees depending on your role and agency policy. Professional liability insurance, also known as malpractice insurance, is provided by the agency for covered clinical activities performed within the scope of your employment. This means that when you are performing authorized patient care duties, the agency's liability insurance covers you. However, you may wish to carry your own individual professional liability policy as additional protection — many professional associations offer affordable coverage. COBRA continuation coverage allows you to temporarily continue your group health insurance if you lose coverage due to a qualifying event such as termination of employment, reduction in hours, or certain other life events. Under COBRA, you pay the full premium plus an administrative fee, but you maintain the same coverage you had as an active employee. The COBRA election period is 60 days from the date of the qualifying event or the date you receive the COBRA notice, whichever is later. COBRA coverage can last up to 18 months for most qualifying events, and up to 36 months for certain dependent qualifying events. Beneficiary designations are important for your life insurance and retirement accounts. During enrollment, you will designate beneficiaries who will receive these benefits in the event of your death. Review and update your beneficiary designations whenever you experience a life change such as marriage, divorce, or the birth of a child. Outdated beneficiary designations can create significant legal and financial complications for your family. All benefit information, plan documents, enrollment forms, and contact information for plan administrators are available through the HR portal and the employee benefits handbook provided during onboarding. If you have questions about any benefit, contact the HR department — they are your primary resource for benefits enrollment, changes, and claims assistance. > **PP SEPARATION NOTICE:** This training module provides education on employee benefits and enrollment. Completion of this module is a Training Module Complete event. Policy acknowledgment is a separate assigned activity in your P&P workflow. ### Knowledge Check 2 **Question:** What is the COBRA election period after a qualifying event? **Answer:** 60 days from the qualifying event date or the date you receive the COBRA notice, whichever is later. COBRA allows temporary continuation of group health coverage at full premium plus an administrative fee. ### Scenario 1 **Setting:** You are a newly eligible employee and your enrollment deadline is Friday. You haven't reviewed the plan options yet because you've been busy with patient visits. **Correct Action:** Prioritize reviewing the plan comparison documents and completing enrollment before the deadline. Missing the deadline means waiting until annual open enrollment — potentially months without coverage. Set aside time this evening or ask HR if you can schedule a brief benefits consultation. **Outcome:** Scenario Practice Complete. ### Scenario 2 **Setting:** You injure your back while transferring a patient. A coworker suggests you use your personal health insurance to avoid the workers' compensation paperwork. **Correct Action:** Decline this advice. Work-related injuries must be reported through the workers' compensation process — not your personal health insurance. Report the injury to your supervisor immediately and complete the required incident documentation. Using personal insurance for a work injury can result in you bearing costs that workers' compensation should cover, and it deprives the agency of injury trend data needed for safety improvements. **Outcome:** Scenario Practice Complete. --- ## COMPETENCY ASSESSMENT — 10 Questions (80% Pass Score) **Q1.** When does health insurance eligibility typically begin for new employees? a) Immediately on the first day of work b) After a waiting period defined in the offer letter (commonly 30, 60, or 90 days) c) After completing the first year of employment d) Only during the January open enrollment **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Eligibility begins after the waiting period specified in the offer letter — commonly 30, 60, or 90 days. **Q2.** A qualifying life event allows you to: a) Cancel your employment contract b) Modify benefit elections outside the annual open enrollment window c) Request a salary increase d) Transfer to a different Care Indeed location **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Qualifying life events (marriage, birth, loss of coverage, etc.) allow mid-year benefit changes within 30 days of the event. **Q3.** FMLA provides eligible employees with up to how many weeks of unpaid leave per year? a) 6 weeks b) 8 weeks c) 12 weeks d) 16 weeks **Correct:** C | **Rationale:** FMLA provides up to 12 weeks of unpaid, job-protected leave per 12-month period for qualifying reasons. **Q4.** If you are injured during a patient visit, which coverage applies? a) Your personal health insurance b) Workers' compensation c) The patient's homeowner's insurance d) Medicare **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Work-related injuries are covered by workers' compensation regardless of fault. **Q5.** The Employee Assistance Program (EAP) is: a) Only available after one year of employment b) A confidential resource at no cost covering counseling, legal, and financial referrals c) Reported to your supervisor for performance tracking d) Only for substance abuse issues **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** EAP is confidential, no-cost, and covers a broad range of services including counseling, legal, financial, and care referrals. **Q6.** Employer match in a 401(k) plan means: a) The employer deducts extra from your paycheck b) The agency contributes additional funds based on your contribution level c) You must contribute the maximum allowed by law d) The match is taxable income in the current year **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Employer match is the agency contributing additional funds to your retirement account based on your contribution — essentially free money. **Q7.** COBRA continuation coverage lasts up to: a) 6 months b) 12 months c) 18 months for most qualifying events d) 24 months **Correct:** C | **Rationale:** COBRA provides up to 18 months of coverage for most qualifying events (up to 36 months for certain dependent events). **Q8.** Under California law, your employer cannot require you to do what in order to use paid sick leave? a) Provide a doctor's note b) Find a substitute to cover your shift c) Use a minimum of 4 hours d) Submit the request in writing **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** California law prohibits employers from requiring employees to find replacement coverage as a condition of using paid sick leave. **Q9.** When should you update your beneficiary designations? a) Only at initial enrollment b) Whenever you experience a life change such as marriage, divorce, or birth of a child c) Every five years d) Only if you change insurance plans **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Beneficiary designations should be updated after any significant life event to ensure benefits are directed correctly. **Q10.** Professional liability insurance provided by Care Indeed covers you when: a) You are performing any healthcare activity anywhere b) You are performing authorized patient care duties within the scope of your employment c) You are volunteering at a community health fair d) You are providing care to family members at home **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Agency-provided professional liability covers authorized duties within the scope of employment only









# GAO-026 — Time & Attendance ## Expanded Module Source — AAA Record v2.0 > **Status:** PASS — Narration QA v2.0 > **Module ID:** GAO-026 > **Title:** Time & Attendance > **Track:** GAO (General Agency Orientation) > **Duration:** 30 minutes (30.0 min narration-defensible) > **Narration Word Count:** 3,900 @ 130 wpm > **Pages:** 2 (TSX canonical) > **Exam Questions:** 10 (5 canonical + 5 expansion) > **Scenarios:** 2 | **Knowledge Checks:** 2 > **passScore:** 80 > **policyMapped:** [] > **policyRefStatus:** needs_review > **Batch:** 3 --- ## PAGE 1 — Timekeeping Requirements: Legal Framework, Agency Policy & Daily Procedures ### Narration Script (~1,950 words) Accurate timekeeping in home health care is not an administrative convenience — it is a legal requirement, a compliance obligation, and a direct reflection of your professional integrity. Every minute you record on your timesheet represents a claim that you were performing authorized work during that time. Inaccurate timekeeping, whether intentional or careless, can result in legal consequences for both you and the agency. The federal Fair Labor Standards Act, FLSA, establishes the baseline requirements for employee compensation. Under the FLSA, non-exempt employees must be paid for all hours worked, including overtime at one and one-half times the regular rate for hours exceeding 40 in a workweek. The FLSA also requires employers to maintain accurate records of hours worked. As a home health employee, you are responsible for accurately recording your time so the agency can meet its legal obligation to pay you correctly. California labor law adds additional protections that exceed federal minimums. California requires overtime pay after 8 hours in a single workday, not just after 40 hours in a week. Double-time pay is required after 12 hours in a single day. California also mandates meal periods and rest breaks: a 30-minute unpaid meal break before the fifth hour of work, and a 10-minute paid rest break for every four hours worked. These are not optional — they are legal requirements. If you work through a meal break, it must be documented and you must be compensated. The agency cannot waive these breaks, and you should not routinely skip them. Travel time between patient homes during your workday is compensable working time. When you leave one patient's home and drive to the next patient's home, that drive time is paid work time. However, your commute from your home to your first patient and from your last patient back to your home is generally not compensable unless specific circumstances apply — such as when you are required to report to the office first or carry supplies from the office to the patient. Document your travel time accurately between visits. Overreporting travel time is timesheet fraud; underreporting it means you are not being paid what you are owed. At Care Indeed, the timekeeping procedure requires you to record the actual time you arrive at a patient's home and the actual time you depart. Do not round your times. If you arrive at 9:07 AM, record 9:07 AM, not 9:00 AM. If you depart at 10:22 AM, record 10:22 AM, not 10:30 AM. Rounding — even by a few minutes — introduces inaccuracy and can constitute falsification when aggregated across visits. Clock-in and clock-out must occur at the actual location of service. If your agency uses a mobile time-tracking application, ensure GPS verification is enabled and that you clock in when you are at the patient's home, not while sitting in your car down the street or at a coffee shop. If the mobile system malfunctions, document your times manually and report the system issue to your supervisor immediately. Overtime must be pre-authorized by your supervisor. You may not work overtime without prior approval except in a patient emergency where leaving would endanger the patient. If an emergency requires you to exceed your scheduled hours, notify your supervisor as soon as possible and document the reason. Unauthorized overtime, even if the work was legitimate, will require explanation and may result in corrective action if it becomes a pattern. If you miss a clock-in or clock-out punch, you must submit a missed punch correction form within 24 hours. The form requires the date, the missed punch time, the reason for the miss, and your supervisor's signature. Do not ask a colleague to clock in or out for you — this is timesheet fraud and is grounds for immediate termination regardless of the circumstances. Timesheet falsification is treated as fraud at Care Indeed. Examples of falsification include: recording hours you did not work, clocking in at a patient's home when you have not yet arrived, documenting a 60-minute visit when you only spent 35 minutes with the patient, having another employee clock in or out on your behalf, altering time records after submission without following the correction procedure, and recording travel time for trips you did not make. Any of these actions can result in immediate termination, repayment of wages, and referral to law enforcement. In the Medicare context, timesheet fraud can also trigger False Claims Act liability if the falsified time is tied to billing. ### Knowledge Check 1 **Question:** You arrive at a patient's home at 9:07 AM but your next available time slot on the app rounds to 9:00 AM. What should you do? **Answer:** Record the actual time of 9:07 AM. Do not round to 9:00 AM. If the system forces rounding, note the actual time in the comments field and report the system limitation to your supervisor. Accuracy in time recording is a legal and agency requirement. --- ## PAGE 2 — Scheduling, Absences, PTO & Professional Expectations ### Narration Script (~1,950 words) Your schedule in home health is driven by patient needs, physician orders, and care plan frequency requirements. Unlike a hospital or clinic where patients come to you, in home health you go to the patient — which means your schedule, your reliability, and your attendance directly affect vulnerable individuals who are counting on you. Pay periods at Care Indeed follow the schedule established by the payroll department. Familiarize yourself with the pay period calendar during your first week. Timesheets must be submitted by the deadline specified for each pay period — late timesheets may delay your pay. Review your pay stub each period to verify that your hours, overtime, mileage reimbursement, and any differentials are correctly reflected. If you identify a discrepancy, report it to payroll within the timeframe specified in the employee handbook. The agency is required to correct payroll errors promptly. Paid time off, or PTO, accrual begins according to the terms of your offer letter and the agency's PTO policy. PTO requests must be submitted in advance through the scheduling system. The agency will make reasonable efforts to approve PTO requests, but patient coverage requirements may necessitate adjustments. You may not use PTO retroactively to cover an unexcused absence unless your supervisor specifically approves the exception. PTO balances, accrual rates, and usage are visible in the HR portal. When you cannot report for a scheduled shift or visit, you must follow the call-in procedure. Notify your supervisor as early as possible — ideally at least two hours before your first scheduled visit. Provide the reason for your absence and an estimated return date. Texting a colleague is not sufficient notification — you must contact your direct supervisor or the on-call coordinator through the designated communication channel. A no-call, no-show — failing to report for a scheduled shift without any notification — is a serious offense. In home health, a no-call/no-show means a homebound patient who was expecting care does not receive it. This is not merely an attendance issue; it is a patient safety issue. A first no-call/no-show results in a written warning. A second occurrence within 12 months may result in final written warning or termination, depending on the circumstances and patient impact. Schedule changes must be coordinated through your supervisor. You may not independently swap visits with another clinician without supervisor approval. Patient-clinician continuity matters for care quality, and unauthorized schedule changes can result in missed visits, documentation gaps, and patient complaints. On-call expectations vary by role and are defined in your position description. If you are assigned to the on-call rotation, you must be available to respond within the timeframe specified — typically within 30 minutes by phone and able to make an in-person visit within the geographic and time parameters established by your supervisor. On-call compensation follows California labor law requirements for on-call pay. Weekend and holiday scheduling is a shared responsibility. Home health patients require care seven days a week, and the agency maintains weekend and holiday coverage rotations. Your participation in weekend and holiday rotations is an expectation of employment. The rotation schedule is published in advance, and requests for specific dates off should be submitted early. Productivity expectations define the number of patient visits or billable hours expected per day or per week for your role. These expectations are based on industry standards, geographic service area, and patient acuity. If you are consistently unable to meet productivity expectations, discuss the barriers with your supervisor — common issues include excessive drive time, documentation backlogs, or patient complexity that requires more time per visit. The agency will work with you to address legitimate barriers, but sustained underperformance without identifiable cause will result in a performance improvement plan. Common timekeeping errors to avoid include: forgetting to clock out after your last visit and having to submit a correction the next day, accidentally recording time in the wrong patient's chart, not documenting travel time and therefore being underpaid, failing to record a meal break as unpaid time, and not verifying your timesheet before submission. A best practice is to review your timesheet at the end of each day before submitting, rather than waiting until the end of the pay period when memories have faded. Let us walk through a practical scenario. You are scheduled for five patient visits today. You arrive at your first patient's home at 8:12 AM and complete the visit at 9:25 AM. You drive 18 minutes to your second patient and arrive at 9:43 AM. Your documentation should reflect: Visit 1 start 8:12 AM, Visit 1 end 9:25 AM. Travel time 9:25 AM to 9:43 AM (18 minutes). Visit 2 start 9:43 AM. Each segment is recorded at actual time, travel time is captured as paid work time, and you have not rounded or estimated any entry. Now consider an absence scenario. You wake up with a fever and cannot work. You call your supervisor at 6:30 AM — one hour before your first visit — explain the situation, and provide your patient list so visits can be reassigned. This is correct procedure. Your patients receive care from a substitute clinician, your supervisor can notify patients of the change, and your absence is documented through the proper channel. Contrast this with simply not showing up: your first patient waits, calls the office, the office scrambles to find coverage, the second patient's medication management visit is delayed, and you receive a no-call/no-show disciplinary action. > **PP SEPARATION NOTICE:** This training module provides education on time and attendance standards. Completion of this module is a Training Module Complete event. Policy acknowledgment is a separate assigned activity in your P&P workflow. ### Knowledge Check 2 **Question:** Under California law, when is overtime pay triggered for a single workday? **Answer:** California requires overtime (1.5x) after 8 hours in a single workday, and double-time (2x) after 12 hours. This is more protective than the federal FLSA, which only triggers overtime after 40 hours in a workweek. ### Scenario 1 **Setting:** You finish your last patient visit at 4:45 PM. Your supervisor has not authorized overtime, but you have 30 minutes of documentation to complete. If you clock out at 4:45 PM and finish documentation at home unpaid, you are working off the clock — a labor law violation. **Correct Action:** Complete the documentation on the clock. If this pushes you past your scheduled end time, notify your supervisor. Working off the clock is illegal under FLSA and California law — the agency is liable for unpaid wages, and you are expected to record all time worked. **Outcome:** Scenario Practice Complete. ### Scenario 2 **Setting:** A colleague is running late and asks you to clock them in at the patient's home using their credentials on the mobile app. **Correct Action:** Decline immediately. Clocking in for another employee is timesheet fraud — grounds for immediate termination for both parties. Direct your colleague to contact the supervisor about their late arrival. **Outcome:** Scenario Practice Complete. --- ## COMPETENCY ASSESSMENT — 10 Questions (80% Pass Score) **Q1.** Under California labor law, overtime pay is triggered after how many hours in a single workday? a) 10 hours b) 8 hours c) 12 hours d) 40 hours **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** California requires overtime (1.5x) after 8 hours in a single workday, which exceeds the federal FLSA 40-hour weekly threshold. **Q2.** Travel time between patient homes during your workday is: a) Not compensable — it's your commute b) Compensable working time that must be recorded c) Only compensable if over 30 minutes d) Compensable only if pre-approved **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Travel between patients during the workday is paid work time under FLSA and California law. **Q3.** You miss a clock-in punch. The correct procedure is to: a) Estimate the time and add it to tomorrow's timesheet b) Submit a missed punch correction form within 24 hours with supervisor signature c) Ask a colleague to vouch for your arrival time d) Subtract the time from your total to be safe **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Missed punches require the formal correction process within 24 hours with supervisor approval. **Q4.** Having another employee clock in or out on your behalf is: a) Acceptable if you're running less than 15 minutes late b) Permitted with supervisor verbal approval c) Timesheet fraud — grounds for immediate termination d) Only a problem if it happens repeatedly **Correct:** C | **Rationale:** Clocking in/out for another employee is timesheet fraud regardless of circumstances. **Q5.** A no-call/no-show in home health is particularly serious because: a) It increases overtime costs b) A homebound patient expecting care does not receive it c) It creates paperwork for the supervisor d) It affects the team morale score **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Home health patients are homebound and depend on scheduled visits — a NCNS is a patient safety issue. **Q6.** Under California law, meal breaks must be provided before which hour of work? a) The fourth hour b) The fifth hour c) The sixth hour d) The eighth hour **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** California requires a 30-minute unpaid meal break before the fifth hour of work. **Q7.** You arrive at a patient's home at 10:13 AM. You should record your arrival time as: a) 10:00 AM b) 10:15 AM c) 10:13 AM d) 10:30 AM **Correct:** C | **Rationale:** Record the actual time — never round. Even small rounding errors constitute falsification when aggregated. **Q8.** Overtime requires: a) No special approval — just work the hours and record them b) Pre-authorization from your supervisor except in patient emergencies c) Written approval from the Administrator d) A minimum of 4 hours beyond the regular shift **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Overtime must be pre-authorized. Patient emergencies are the exception — notify supervisor as soon as possible. **Q9.** If you identify a discrepancy on your pay stub, you should: a) Wait until the next pay period to see if it self-corrects b) Report it to payroll within the specified timeframe for correction c) Adjust your next timesheet to compensate d) Discuss it with coworkers before reporting **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** Report payroll discrepancies promptly through the proper channel. The agency is obligated to correct errors. **Q10.** Working off the clock — completing documentation at home after clocking out — is: a) Expected for efficient employees b) A violation of FLSA and California labor law c) Acceptable if it's less than 30 minutes d) Only a problem for hourly employees **Correct:** B | **Rationale:** All time worked must be recorded and compensated. Working off the clock violates federal and California labor law.







# GAO-025 — Documentation Standards Overview ## Expanded Module Source — AAA Record v2.0 > **Status:** PASS — Narration QA v2.0 > **Module ID:** GAO-025 > **Title:** Documentation Standards Overview > **Track:** GAO (General Agency Orientation) > **Duration:** 30 minutes (30.0 min narration-defensible) > **Narration Word Count:** 3,900 @ 130 wpm > **Pages:** 2 (TSX canonical) > **Exam Questions:** 10 (5 canonical + 5 expansion) > **Scenarios:** 2 | **Knowledge Checks:** 2 > **passScore:** 80 > **policyMapped:** ['CL-CD-001'] > **policyRefStatus:** needs_review > **Batch:** 3 --- ## PAGE 1 — Documentation as the Legal Record: Standards, Formats & Requirements ### Narration Script (~1,950 words) Documentation in home health care is not paperwork — it is the legal record of every service you provide, every clinical decision you make, and every patient interaction you conduct. In a surveyor's eyes, if it is not documented, it did not happen. This principle governs everything from how you record a blood pressure reading to how you justify the skilled need for continued home health services. The Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services, under 42 CFR Section 484.110, requires that clinical records be maintained for every patient receiving home health services. These records must be complete, accurate, and contemporaneous — meaning documented at or near the time of the event. The Accreditation Commission for Health Care, ACHC, reinforces these standards and adds specific expectations for documentation timeliness, authentication, and content quality. At Care Indeed, our documentation standards are governed by policy CL-CD-001. This policy establishes the expectations every clinician must follow regardless of discipline. Whether you are a registered nurse completing a Start of Care assessment, a physical therapist documenting a treatment session, or a home health aide recording vital signs, the same foundational standards apply. Let us begin with why documentation matters beyond compliance. First, documentation is a legal document. In any malpractice claim, workers' compensation dispute, or regulatory investigation, your clinical notes are the primary evidence. Courts and administrative law judges rely on what was written, not what you remember saying or doing months later. Second, documentation drives reimbursement. Medicare pays for home health services based on what is documented. If your visit note does not clearly establish skilled need, homebound status, and medical necessity, the claim may be denied or the agency may face recoupment. Third, documentation ensures continuity of care. In home health, multiple clinicians visit the same patient across days and weeks. Your documentation is how the next clinician knows what happened, what changed, and what the current plan requires. The 24-hour documentation standard is a critical benchmark. At Care Indeed, all visit documentation must be completed within 24 hours of the visit. This is not merely a suggestion — it is an agency standard enforced through quality audits. Late documentation introduces risk: memory fades, details become inaccurate, and the record loses its contemporaneous reliability. If you cannot complete documentation on the same day as the visit, you must complete it by the end of the following business day and note the reason for the delay. Now let us discuss documentation formats. The two primary narrative formats used in home health are SOAP and DAR. SOAP stands for Subjective, Objective, Assessment, and Plan. The subjective section captures what the patient reports — symptoms, complaints, concerns in their own words. The objective section records your clinical findings — vital signs, wound measurements, range of motion, functional observations. The assessment section is your professional clinical judgment — what the data means, whether the patient is improving, stable, or declining. The plan section documents what you will do next — continue current interventions, modify the care plan, refer to another discipline, or contact the physician. DAR stands for Data, Action, Response. This format is used for focused documentation of specific events or interventions. Data captures the relevant clinical findings. Action records what you did. Response documents how the patient responded to your intervention. Regardless of format, all documentation must be objective and measurable. Instead of writing the patient is doing better, write the patient ambulated 150 feet with a rolling walker, demonstrating improved gait stability compared to 100 feet on the previous visit. Instead of writing wound looks good, write wound bed is 90 percent granulating, no signs of infection, wound dimensions reduced from 3.2 by 2.1 centimeters to 2.8 by 1.8 centimeters. Measurable documentation supports skilled need, tracks progress toward goals, and withstands survey scrutiny. Avoid prohibited abbreviations. The Joint Commission and ACHC maintain lists of abbreviations that must not be used because they create ambiguity. For example, U for units can be mistaken for zero. QD can be misread as QID. IU for international units can be misread as IV. At Care Indeed, use only approved abbreviations listed in the agency's approved abbreviation reference. Every visit note must justify the skilled need for the service provided. Medicare does not pay for maintenance-only services unless they require the skills of a licensed professional to be performed safely and effectively. Your documentation must clearly articulate why a skilled clinician was needed for this specific visit — what clinical assessment, skilled intervention, patient education, or care coordination occurred that could not have been performed by a non-skilled individual. Homebound status must be documented at every visit. The patient's homebound status is a fundamental Medicare eligibility criterion. Your note must describe the specific condition, functional limitation, or medical restriction that confines the patient to the home. Leaving the home requires considerable and taxing effort is the standard — document what makes it taxing for this specific patient. ### Knowledge Check 1 **Question:** A visit note states "Patient is doing better." Why is this problematic? **Answer:** The statement is subjective and non-measurable. Documentation must include objective, quantifiable data such as specific functional improvements, vital sign trends, or wound measurements to support clinical conclusions and justify skilled need. --- ## PAGE 2 — Orders, Corrections, EHR Practices & Documentation Integrity ### Narration Script (~1,950 words) Physician orders are the legal authority for every service you provide in the patient's home. No service may be initiated, modified, or discontinued without a valid physician order. At Care Indeed, physician orders follow a specific documentation protocol that every clinician must understand. When you receive a verbal order — for example, a physician calls to change a medication dose during your visit — you must document the order immediately using the read-back verification method. Write down the order exactly as communicated, read it back to the physician verbatim, and obtain verbal confirmation that you read it back correctly. Document the order in the patient record including the date, time, physician name, the exact order content, and the notation verbal order read back and confirmed. Verbal orders must be authenticated by the physician's signature within the timeframe required by your agency policy and state regulation — typically within 30 days for Medicare. Standing orders and protocol orders are not permitted in home health. Every order must be patient-specific. You cannot apply a blanket protocol to a patient without an individualized physician order. If a patient's condition changes and you need new orders, you must contact the physician, obtain the specific order, and document it before implementing the change — except in an emergency where immediate action is needed to prevent harm, in which case you act first and obtain the order as soon as possible afterward. Late entries and addendums are sometimes necessary but must follow strict standards. A late entry is documentation added after the 24-hour window. It must be clearly labeled as a late entry with the current date and time, and must reference the original date of the event being documented. An addendum is additional information added to an existing note. It must be clearly labeled as an addendum, reference the original note date, and include the current date, time, and your signature. Never alter, backdate, or delete an existing entry. Altering documentation after the fact can constitute fraud. Error correction in paper records requires drawing a single line through the error so the original text remains readable, writing the correction above or beside the error, and initialing and dating the correction. Never use white-out, erasure, or heavy black marks that obscure the original text. In electronic records, the EHR system should maintain an audit trail of all changes. If you need to correct an electronic entry, use the amendment or addendum function — never delete the original entry. Electronic health record practices require additional vigilance. When documenting in the EHR, ensure you are in the correct patient chart before entering any data. Verify the patient name, date of birth, and medical record number. Use your own unique login credentials — never share passwords or document under another user's account. Log out of the system when you step away from the device. Do not copy and paste previous notes without carefully reviewing and updating the content — copy-paste errors are a leading cause of documentation inaccuracy and are specifically targeted during surveys. Authentication and co-signature requirements vary by discipline and licensure. All entries must be signed with your full legal name, professional credentials, and the date and time. Students, LVNs documenting certain assessments, and home health aides have specific co-signature requirements where a supervising clinician must review and authenticate the documentation. At Care Indeed, co-signatures must be completed within the timeframe specified in your discipline-specific policy. Documentation integrity is a compliance and legal obligation. Falsifying documentation — including documenting services not provided, inflating time spent, recording inaccurate clinical findings, or backdating entries — constitutes fraud under the False Claims Act and can result in criminal prosecution, civil penalties, termination, and exclusion from federal healthcare programs. Even unintentional inaccuracies can create liability. Your professional license depends on the integrity of your documentation. Common documentation deficiencies cited in CMS and ACHC surveys include: failure to document homebound status at each visit, missing or incomplete physician orders, lack of skilled need justification, use of prohibited abbreviations, unsigned or unauthenticated entries, copy-paste errors with outdated clinical information, failure to document patient education and response, missing wound measurements or vital sign trends, late documentation beyond the 24-hour standard, and failure to document communication with the physician or interdisciplinary team. Let us apply these standards to a practical home health scenario. You are a registered nurse visiting Mrs. Rodriguez for a skilled nursing assessment. During the visit, you notice her blood pressure is significantly elevated at 178/102, she reports new onset headache and blurred vision, and her medication bottle shows she has not been taking her antihypertensive medication. Your documentation must include: the objective vital signs with exact readings, the patient's subjective report in her own words, your assessment of the clinical significance, the action you took — which should include contacting the physician immediately — the physician's verbal order with read-back verification, the patient education you provided about medication adherence, the patient's response to your teaching, and the plan for follow-up. This complete documentation tells the clinical story, justifies the skilled need, protects the patient, and satisfies every survey standard. Another common scenario involves the home health aide. You are completing a routine personal care visit and notice the patient has a new skin tear on their forearm that was not present at your last visit. Even though you are an aide, not a nurse, you must document what you observed — the location, approximate size, and appearance of the skin tear — and report it to the supervising RN immediately. Your documentation should read something like: Observed new skin tear on left forearm approximately 2 centimeters in length with minor bleeding. Area cleaned and bandaged per standing wound care supplies. RN supervisor notified at 10:45 AM. This documents your observation, your action within scope, and your communication to the appropriate clinical authority. Remember: Documentation is care. If your documentation is incomplete, inaccurate, or late, you have not fully provided the service — because the service includes the record of what you did. Every entry you make becomes part of a legal document that may be reviewed by surveyors, auditors, attorneys, physicians, and other clinicians. Write every note as if a judge will read it — because someday, one might. > **PP SEPARATION NOTICE:** Policy Reference: CL-CD-001 (needs_review). This training module provides education on documentation standards. Completion of this module is a Training Module Complete event. Policy acknowledgment is a separate assigned activity in your P&P workflow. ### Knowledge Check 2 **Question:** You realize at 9 PM that you forgot to document a verbal order received during your 2 PM visit. What is the correct procedure? **Answer:** Complete a late entry clearly labeled with the current date/time, referencing the original visit date and time of the verbal order. Include the full verbal order content, physician name, and read-back verification notation. Never backdate the entry. ### Scenario 1 **Setting:** You are an RN documenting a wound care visit. Your previous note from last week was detailed and well-written. You consider copying and pasting it and just changing the wound measurements. **Correct Action:** Do not copy-paste the previous note. Write a fresh note reflecting today's specific findings, today's patient presentation, today's interventions, and today's patient response. Copy-paste errors introduce inaccurate information from previous visits and are a top survey deficiency. **Outcome:** Scenario Practice Complete. ### Scenario 2 **Setting:** A colleague asks to use your EHR login to document a visit because their account is locked out. **Correct Action:** Decline. Each clinician must document under their own unique credentials. Sharing login information violates authentication standards, HIPAA security rules, and agency policy.
